#### Solved PYQ 9 · 2025 · Q2(a) · 20 marks

**Question:** Present a detailed account of the debate between Gandhi and Ambedkar on the issue of caste discrimination.

##### Demand decoding

| Element | Requirement |
|---|---|
| Directive | "Present a detailed account of the debate" — the **disagreement** is the object, not the two thinkers |
| Core issue | On which axes they differ, and what follows |
| Scope boundary | Twenty marks: five or six axes run in parallel, convergence, the 1932 test, adjudication |
| Key distinction | Reform of the practice against annihilation of the sanction |
| Non-negotiables | No two sequential biographies; no equal division of credit |

##### Independent model answer

**Position.** ⚠️ The debate is a disagreement about the **object** of reform, and it must be displayed on shared axes.

**Axis one — the primary evil.** ✅ For Gandhi, untouchability and moral degradation, and later the wider birth barriers; for Ambedkar, caste itself as graded inequality, an ascending scale of reverence and a descending scale of contempt.

**Axis two — the hereditary duty-order.** ✅ Gandhi earlier defended an idealised duty-order as duties without rank, and moved later towards opposing birth barriers and supporting inter-caste marriage; Ambedkar holds birth-based division intrinsically unjust throughout. ⚠️ The residue is that inherited allocation survives the denial of rank.

**Axis three — the root mechanism.** ✅ Corrupted religion, prejudice and lost conscience against endogamy, scriptural sanction, social closure and power — which is why Gandhi's remedy begins with conduct and Ambedkar's with the marriage circle and the authority that legitimates rank.

**Axis four — the agent.** ✅ The transformed conscience of the privileged, with oppressed participation, against the **autonomous** organisation and representation of the oppressed themselves.

**Axis five — method.** ✅ Self-purification, truth-force, constructive work of education, sanitation and common service, and reform from within, against rights, law, education, organisation, representation and inter-caste marriage.

**Axis six — religion and democracy.** ✅ Reform from within and ethical fellowship in self-rule, against rejection of caste-sanctioning authority, conversion in **1956**, constitutional morality and social democracy.

**The 1932 test case.** ⚠️ The Communal Award provided separate electoral arrangements; Gandhi opposed them and fasted; the Poona Pact substituted reserved seats in joint electorates with a primary-election mechanism and increased reserved seats. The question was **who may choose a subordinated group's representatives**, and the settlement was reached under coercive circumstances.

**Convergence.** ✅ Both hold untouchability indefensible, affirm equal worth, regard political freedom without social reform as incomplete, and reject violence as a foundation.

**Adjudication.** ⚠️ Convergence identifies common ends and does not equalise diagnostic adequacy. Ambedkar's account explains what Gandhi's cannot: why the hierarchy survives every reform of its outward practice. Gandhi nonetheless secures what Ambedkar's method cannot compel — the responsibility of the privileged, the identity of means and ends, and the insistence that law must enter social character — and Ambedkar himself holds safeguards without fraternity to remain formal. The defensible position is therefore **Ambedkarite in structure and rights, Gandhian only where moral transformation serves — never substitutes for — annihilation**.

##### Why this earns marks

**Answer-writing focus:** this is the stem on which the two-biography structure costs most, because a "detailed account of the debate" is marked on whether the disagreement is visible. Labelled axes make it visible in the layout itself, and the 1932 episode earns its place only as the point where the axes produce incompatible answers rather than as narrative.

**Exam-length execution — 20 marks, about 330 to 400 words:** position one line; six axes ten lines; the 1932 test case four lines; convergence two lines; adjudication four lines. Compression plan: merge axes three and five before dropping the adjudication, which the directive requires.

- **Structure:** position → six labelled axes → 1932 test case → convergence → adjudication.
- **Doctrine:** the comparison matrix; convergence without false equivalence.
- **Evidence:** the 1932 Communal Award and Poona Pact, interpreted; conversion in 1956.
- **Distinction:** reform of the practice against annihilation of the sanction.
- **Criticism:** paternalism and structural insufficiency against legalism and the limits of state action.

##### How to improve this answer

Add one sentence stating what Gandhi secured that Ambedkar's method cannot compel — the responsibility of the privileged, the identity of means and ends, and the insistence that law must enter social character — because an adjudication that concedes nothing reads as advocacy rather than judgment. If two further lines are available, note that Ambedkar himself holds safeguards without fraternity to remain formal, which shows the concession is internal to his position rather than a courtesy extended to his opponent. Do **not** assert figures, negotiating detail or an account of the fast beyond the sequence stated; the philosophical content lies entirely in the principle at stake.

### ORIGINAL MAINS PRACTICE WITH MODEL SOLUTIONS

⚠️ These six originals are constructed to cover the parts of the owned clause that the 2018–2025 corpus has **not** yet examined directly, so that preparation is not confined to the nine questions already asked. They test the conceptual grammar and the four-term separation, the division-of-labourers argument against the functional defence, the four dimensions of caste injustice, the place of fraternity in the triad, the village-and-modernity dispute, and the four justifications of affirmative action with the reverse-discrimination objection.

#### Original 1 · 10 marks · about 150 to 220 words

**Question:** "Untouchability is a symptom of caste, not a synonym for it." Examine the significance of this distinction.

##### Independent model answer

**Position.** ⚠️ The distinction is not terminological housekeeping; it decides what a remedy must reach.

**The four terms.** ✅ The textual fourfold order is a normative classification and is not identical with the lived order; the lived birth-group is a locally organised, generally endogamous group, of which there are thousands with wide regional variation; the caste system is the institutional order joining birth, marriage closure, ranked status, allocated occupation and restricted association; and untouchability names practices imposing extreme exclusion and stigma on designated groups.

**Why untouchability is a symptom.** ✅ The ranking principle — that birth fixes worth — operates throughout the order. Untouchability is its most extreme expression, so a rule abolishing the practice can succeed while birth continues to allocate marriage, occupation and standing everywhere else.

**The doctrinal consequence.** ✅ This is why Ambedkar holds that untouchability cannot be eliminated without annihilating caste, and why reform limited to the practice is rejected as insufficient.

**The evaluative consequence.** ⚠️ It also fixes the terms of the Gandhi–Ambedkar dispute: a position can condemn untouchability without opposing an idealised duty-order that still allocates by birth.

**Verdict.** ⚠️ Merging the terms makes both positions unstatable and converts a structural argument into a moral one.

#### Original 2 · 10 marks · about 150 to 220 words

**Question:** Is caste best understood as a division of labour? Give reasons for your answer.

##### Independent model answer

**Position.** ⚠️ No — and stating **why** requires a distinction rather than a denial.

**The defence, at its strongest.** ✅ Every complex society divides work; specialisation is efficient; caste was the traditional Indian form of that division, so condemning it appears to condemn specialisation itself.

**The decisive distinction.** ✅ A division of **labour** allocates tasks and permits entry, exit and judgment by aptitude, leaving the worker's standing untouched. A division of **labourers** allocates and ranks persons: it fixes occupation by birth, ignores aptitude and choice, subdivides the labouring population into hierarchically graded compartments, and requires a closed marriage circle to keep them distinct.

**Three supporting moves.** ✅ Efficiency cannot license ascription, and an allocation ignoring aptitude is not efficient on its own terms; the ranking is of persons, which no functional account of tasks requires; and stigma attaches to the labour **because of who performs it**, so the hierarchy rather than the division produces the degradation.

**The conceded limit.** ⚠️ The argument does not reach the ritual and purity dimension, so used alone it yields an economic reading that cannot explain why exclusion feels obligatory.

**Verdict.** ⚠️ Caste is a hereditary ranking of workers, and must be paired with graded inequality and the sanction.

#### Original 3 · 15 marks · about 250 to 330 words

**Question:** Examine the claim that remedies for caste injustice must satisfy redistribution, recognition and representation together.

##### Independent model answer

**Position.** ⚠️ The claim is defensible as a statement of **non-substitutability** and overstated if read as a claim about priority.

**The four dimensions.** ✅ Redistribution answers unequal land, labour, education, networks and security, and requires resources and institutional access. Recognition answers stigma, purity and pollution, humiliation and graded worth, and requires the repudiation of inherited rank. Representation answers the condition in which others speak for a group and control candidate and office access, and requires autonomous voice and political safeguards. Religion answers hierarchy presented as sacred authority, and requires freedom of conscience together with the rejection of civic disability.

**Why they are not substitutes.** ✅ Each names a distinct mechanism, so a remedy succeeding in one can leave the others intact. **Identity without redistribution** leaves material power exactly where it was; **redistribution without recognition** transfers resources while preserving humiliation. Both failures are observable in principle and neither is describable if the dimensions are merged.

**The two mediating axes.** ✅ Endogamy makes caste continuity depend on regulating women's marriage and sexuality, so caste and gender are **jointly structured** rather than arithmetically added; and caste interacts with control of land, labour and education without being reducible to class, since no account of ownership supplies the sacred sanction.

**The objection.** ✅ The grid multiplies categories: all four are ultimately about power, so dividing power into four components describes one domination four times. **Reply:** ⚠️ the division earns its keep by predicting failure, which an undifferentiated concept of power cannot state.

**The residual limit.** ❓ The grid establishes no **priority**; whether recognition must precede redistribution is contested and unresolved here.

**Verdict.** ⚠️ Together, yes — as a completeness test on any proposed remedy, not as a sequence.

#### Original 4 · 15 marks · about 250 to 330 words

**Question:** "Fraternity is the least attended and most necessary of Ambedkar's three principles." Critically discuss.

##### Independent model answer

**Position.** ⚠️ The claim is correct about necessity and requires care about what fraternity is.

**The triad as a structure.** ✅ Liberty without equality permits domination by the already powerful; equality without liberty suppresses individuality; both without fraternity lack the social disposition to recognise others as equals at all. The three are not parallel goods but mutual conditions.

**What fraternity names.** ✅ Not sentimentality or goodwill, but the **social basis of democracy** — a sense of common associated life among social equals, supported by rights and institutions. It is therefore not the alternative to rights but what rights require in order to function.

**Why it is most necessary here.** ✅ Caste is ranked separation: it narrows loyalty to the birth-group and obstructs public spirit, so the disposition to common action among persons who do not know one another is exactly what the order withdraws. ✅ A political democracy resting on graded social worth is unstable — one person one vote and one vote one value against unequal worth — and the instability is a deficit of fraternity, not of procedure.

**The objection.** ✅ The concept is too vague to do this work: if it cannot be legislated or measured, any failure can be attributed to its absence and any success to its presence. **Reply:** ⚠️ it is testable where caste operates — whether association across the boundary is free, and whether common action extends beyond the birth-group.

**The residual limit.** ❓ Cultivation remains unspecified: no quota produces fraternity, and the owner does not say how it is produced.

**Verdict.** ⚠️ It is most necessary precisely because it is the element no safeguard delivers — which is why safeguards without it remain formal.

#### Original 5 · 20 marks · about 330 to 400 words

**Question:** "Neither the village nor centralised modernity is inherently emancipatory for caste." Examine this claim with reference to Gandhi and Ambedkar.

##### Independent model answer

**Position.** ⚠️ The claim is correct, and its force lies in replacing a contrast of eras with a **test**.

**The two positions.** ✅ Gandhi treats decentralised community as a possible school of responsibility, meaningful labour and self-rule: it is where conduct is enacted daily, and his constructive programme of education, sanitation and common service presupposes exactly that setting. ✅ Ambedkar warns that the **actually existing** village can concentrate caste authority, dependency and exclusion — precisely because face-to-face dependence makes exit costly and observation constant.

**Why this is not tradition against modernity.** ⚠️ The disagreement is not about eras. Centralised modern institutions can reproduce caste through recruitment, networks and the informal transmission of standing; local community can reproduce it through dependence and surveillance. Neither scale carries emancipation as a property.

**The test that replaces the contrast.** ✅ Ask whether scale, technology and community enlarge **mobility**, **equal status**, **constitutional rights** and **exit from inherited occupation**. That test is applied to both, and it is what makes the claim examinable rather than rhetorical.

**Running the test.** ⚠️ Gandhi's setting scores on shared practice, since common service attacks the ritual rule that certain labour pollutes those who perform it; it scores poorly on exit, because dependence is unchanged and there is nowhere to go. Ambedkar's institutional route scores on rights and exit, since a benefit that can be withdrawn is not a right and representation supplies voice; it scores poorly on everyday relations, which is why he insists that constitutional morality and fraternity must accompany the machinery.

**Objection and reply.** ✅ The test is impossible to apply, since mobility and status are not measurable. **Reply:** ⚠️ it is applied comparatively rather than absolutely — whether an arrangement enlarges or contracts each of the four — and the owner asserts no measurement of any Indian case.

**Verdict.** ⚠️ Neither scale is emancipatory without anti-caste power. The village is not condemned as such and modernity is not credited as such; what decides is whether either arrangement opens the marriage circle, dissolves dependence and secures rights that do not depend on goodwill.

#### Original 6 · 20 marks · about 330 to 400 words

**Question:** On what ground, if any, is affirmative action for historically excluded castes justified? Meet the strongest objection to your position.

##### Independent model answer

**Position.** ⚠️ Name the ground first. Mine is **compensatory in ground, distributive in mechanism, representational in institutional justification, and anti-domination in ultimate aim** — and the join must be stated, because the four grounds have different premises and different termination conditions.

**The compensatory argument, reconstructed.** ✅ Caste exclusion was systematic, institutional and for long periods sanctioned by religious authority and by law; it was **group-directed**, operating on persons by reason of birth-membership rather than conduct; its effects are transmitted through inherited wealth, land, occupation, schooling, language, networks and social confidence; removing the formal bar from today therefore ratifies those results as though they were the outcome of fair competition; and a remedy must reach the transmission mechanism and must be group-directed because the disadvantage is. ⚠️ The presupposition — that institutionally caused disadvantage can be traced reliably enough to justify a group-directed remedy — must be argued, not assumed.

**Ambedkar's stronger framing.** ⚠️ On his diagnosis caste is **graded inequality**, an ascending scale of reverence and descending scale of contempt, so the wrong is a hierarchy of **worth** rather than a distributive shortfall — which is why his argument runs through representation and power rather than welfare.

**The strongest objection.** ✅ Persons must be treated as individuals; selection should track qualification; reservation allocates by ascriptive membership; the excluded individual is personally innocent; so a fresh injustice is inflicted, reproducing allocation by birth-group.

**The reply, in four moves.** ⚠️ **Symmetry:** the unreserved baseline is not neutral, since qualifications are themselves unequally producible. **Merit as proxy:** merit is functional capacity for the role and examination performance is a proxy whose reliability varies with coaching, nutrition, language and schooling — refining the proxy pursues merit more accurately rather than abandoning it. **Advantage, not guilt:** no culpability is attributed; the claim is that some are unjustly advantaged by a continuing distribution. **Group harm, group remedy:** a group-transmitted disadvantage cannot be reached individually.

**The conceded residue.** ⚠️ Over-inclusion of the already advantaged, which the creamy-layer filter addresses at the cost of weakening the representational ground; and cost falling unevenly near the margin.

**Verdict.** ⚠️ Justified on the stated join; the objection is answered but not eliminated, and saying so is stronger than pretending otherwise.

## OPTIONAL ADVANCED DEPTH — NOT REQUIRED FOR A CORE ANSWER

> ⚠️ **Status.** Everything below is enrichment drawn from the optional advanced dossier. **No** owned 2018–2025 previous-year part requires any of it, and every one of the nine owned parts is answerable in full from the Basic block alone. The dossier's own deployment rule applies: in a 15- or 20-marker use **at most one** item from this block unless the stem itself asks for an advanced contrast, and let core exposition, PYQ-fit structure, standard objection–reply and a direct conclusion occupy the majority of the script.

### A1. Moral reform against structural reconstruction — the residual dispute

⚠️ **The contention.** Gandhian heart-change and reform within society, set against the Ambedkarite insistence on legal, political and social restructuring. **Why it stays optional:** the Basic block already makes this comparison central, and Sessions 11 and 18 run it to an adjudication. The dossier adds nothing the owned parts require; what it supplies is a way of holding the dispute open where a stem explicitly invites an unresolved verdict.

### A2. Caste as status hierarchy against caste as labour order — the reading dispute

⚠️ **The contention.** Some readings foreground ritual and status closure; others stress property, labour and material domination. **Why it stays optional:** useful for a deeper analysis of graded inequality, and not required beyond the owner's coverage. ⚠️ **Deployment caution:** the second reading must never be allowed to slide into the reduction of caste to class, which the Basic block forbids at Sessions 4, 7 and 17. Used correctly, it sharpens the point that the labour argument does not reach the ritual dimension.

### A3. Constitutional morality against social fraternity — the sufficiency dispute

⚠️ **The contention.** Institutional safeguards can discipline hierarchy; the rival concern says law alone cannot create fraternity. **Why it stays optional:** it is a strong evaluation device once the Gandhi–Ambedkar basics are secure, and Session 15 already concedes that fraternity cannot be legislated. The advanced version presses the further question of whether a diffused public commitment is itself producible by institutional means — a question the owner leaves open.

### A4. Ambedkar beyond Marx, and Marx beyond Ambedkar

⚠️ **The contention.** Class analysis diagnoses exploitation; the Ambedkarite view says caste has an autonomous social psychology and political structure. **Why it stays optional:** it helps in Indian comparison answers, but Marx himself is owned by Political Ideologies, and Session 17 already states the four-axis contrast with the precise point that economic power is accepted while the reduction of caste to class is denied.

### A5. The strongest additional objection and reply

- **Objection.** ✅ Ambedkarite institutional remedies risk **legalism** if everyday caste consciousness remains intact: rights are enforced by institutions staffed from the same society, and a safeguard operating inside an unchanged social order may be complied with formally and evaded practically.
- **Reply.** ⚠️ His framework is not mere legalism. **Constitutional morality** requires social pedagogy, fraternity, representation and transformed self-respect; the law is necessary precisely **because** moral reform alone proved unreliable, not because law was expected to work alone.
- **Residual limit.** ❓ How the diffused public commitment is produced remains unspecified, and the objection therefore survives as a question about cultivation rather than as a refutation.

### A6. India-facing deployment that stays inside the fence

⚠️ Use representation, education, the land and labour hierarchy, inter-caste marriage, village power and constitutional democracy **only** to show how caste persists as graded inequality despite formal equality, and keep Gandhi and Ambedkar as the controlling frame throughout. ❌ Do not let later caste theory displace the two named thinkers; ❌ do not convert the section into movement chronology or policy statistics; ❌ do not sentimentalise "harmony" without addressing structure.

### A7. Promotion trigger and source trail

⚠️ **Promotion trigger:** this material is promoted into Core only if a previous-year part directly asks about Phule, Periyar, contemporary Dalit theory, the caste-as-class comparison, or constitutional morality as the main theme. As checked against the 2018–2025 corpus, **no such part exists**, so the block remains optional in this generation.

✅ **Credible source trail recorded by the dossier:** Gandhi, *Hind Swaraj* and writings in *Harijan*; Ambedkar, *Annihilation of Caste*, "Castes in India", *States and Minorities* and Constituent Assembly speeches; Christophe Jaffrelot, *Dr Ambedkar and Untouchability*; Eleanor Zelliot, *From Untouchable to Dalit*; Anand Teltumbde, *The Persistence of Caste*; Sharmila Rege, *Against the Madness of Manu*. ❌ No page, chapter, edition or verbatim wording is asserted for any of these, and none is quoted.

## CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

> These are compressed revision notes for the complete topic, placed last. They cover every teaching session and both practice blocks, and they are organised by the topic's own structure rather than by a generic notes template. The Basic material and the Optional-Advanced residue are kept visibly separate, and the ASCII master flow diagram closes the package as part R.

### A. Say it exactly — the definition bank

- **Caste discrimination** — the unjust allocation of status, occupation, association, resources and political voice by hereditary birth-group membership, sustained by a closed marriage circle and legitimated as sacred obligation.
- **Textual fourfold order (*varṇa*)** — a normative classification in Brahmanical texts; **not** identical with the lived order.
- **Lived birth-group (*jāti*)** — a birth-based, generally endogamous, locally organised group; thousands of them, wide regional variation.
- **Caste system** — the institutional order joining birth, endogamy, status, occupation and social closure; not a division of labour, not reducible to class.
- **Untouchability** — practices imposing extreme exclusion and stigma on designated groups; a **symptom and intensification** of hierarchy, not its whole.
- **Endogamy** — the enforced marriage boundary that carries membership across generations; the condition of the group's continued existence.
- **Graded inequality** — an ascending scale of reverence and a descending scale of contempt in which each stratum has a stake in the order's continuance.
- **Division of labourers** — the hereditary ranking of workers themselves rather than of tasks.
- **Annihilation of caste** — destruction of the sanctified authority that ranks and separates, not reform of its outward practice.
- **Constitutional morality** — a diffused public commitment to constitutional norms, required because the form of administration can be perverted without altering the text.
- **Social democracy** — liberty, equality and fraternity held as a way of life; democracy as a mode of associated living.
- **Fraternity** — the social basis of democracy: common associated life among social **equals**, supported by rights and institutions. Not sentimentality.
- **Navayāna** — the reconstruction of Buddhism as an ethical-social practice of reason, compassion, equality and fraternity.

### B. The architecture — open almost every answer here

1. **Name the contest** the stem is in — mechanism, method, comparison or persistence.
2. **Define with the mechanism**, never with "social stratification": graded, birth-based social closure sustained by a closed marriage circle.
3. **Deploy the separation** the contest turns on (Part D).
4. **Argue** — named anchor → analysis → objection → reply → **residue**.
5. **Close with a graded verdict**, never a social-justice slogan.

⚠️ **The master thesis, once:** moral transformation reaches the conscience of the dominant, which law cannot compel; rights and representation reach the structure of power, which conscience cannot be relied upon to surrender. Social democracy requires both; only the second can be institutionally secured.

### C. The four contests and the nine owned parts

| Contest | Question | Owned parts |
|---|---|---|
| Mechanism | what reproduces caste — work, class or rank? | 2024 Q1(c) 10 |
| Method | reform of the practice or annihilation of the sanction? | 2022 Q2(b) 15 · 2023 Q3(b) 15 · 2021 Q4(a) 20 |
| Comparison | do the two thinkers differ, and on which axis? | 2019 Q4(a) 20 · 2025 Q2(a) 20 |
| Persistence | why does caste survive equal citizenship? | 2018 Q1(c) 10 · 2020 Q3(a) 20 · 2022 Q1(b) 10 |

⚠️ **Corpus share:** 9 of 112 Socio-Political parts, present in every year 2018–2025 and twice in 2022. Not one asks for a history of caste, a description of practices, a survey of movements, or a policy evaluation of reservation on its own.

### D. Four terms, four scopes — and the five separations

**The four terms:** normative textual classification · lived endogamous birth-group · the institutional order of birth, rank, occupation and closure · the extreme practices of exclusion.

**The five separations that must never merge:**

1. *varṇa* ≠ *jāti* ≠ caste ≠ untouchability.
2. Division of labour ≠ division of labourers.
3. Reform of untouchability ≠ annihilation of caste.
4. Enactment ≠ notification ≠ enforcement ≠ social transformation.
5. Assertion by oppressed castes ≠ defence of hereditary supremacy.

**The two wrecks:** *flattening* the terms, which erases the disagreement; *freezing* the institution as unchanged across all history, which makes reform and its failure inexplicable.

**The discriminating test:** if the outward practice is reformed while birth still allocates marriage, rank, occupation and social worth, the sanction has survived the reform.

### E. The reproduction engine and the intervention map

**Chain:** endogamy → hereditary status → occupational closure → purity and pollution → religious sanction → social and economic power → (ambivalently) political mobilisation ⇒ **graded inequality**.

**Property:** each link makes the next possible, so the system **absorbs the reform of any single component**.

| Remedy | Link reached |
|---|---|
| Inter-caste marriage | the marriage circle |
| Reservation | occupational closure and access |
| Article 17 | the ritual moralisation of distance |
| Annihilation | the sanction that legitimates rank |
| Land, credit, education | material dependence |
| Autonomous organisation | converts mobilisation into assertion |

⚠️ This is a **reproduction** account, not a genesis account, and it identifies intervention points rather than a ranking of causes.

### F. Division of labourers against the functional defence

| Division of labour | Division of labourers |
|---|---|
| allocates tasks | allocates and ranks **persons** |
| permits entry and exit | fixes occupation by birth |
| tracks aptitude and choice | ignores aptitude and choice |
| leaves standing untouched | attaches hereditary worth to the worker |
| compatible with free association | requires a closed marriage circle |

**Three moves:** efficiency cannot license ascription, and ascription is not efficient; the ranking is of persons in hierarchically graded compartments; stigma follows the **worker**, not the work.

⚠️ **Conceded limit:** the argument does not reach the ritual and purity dimension. Pair it with graded inequality and the sanction, or it yields an economic reading that cannot explain why exclusion feels obligatory.

### G. Graded inequality and the failure of solidarity

- Not a binary: an **ascending scale of reverence** and a **descending scale of contempt**.
- Each level is subordinated above and superior below, so combination with those beneath costs a relative standing.
- **Consequence:** solidarity is against a manufactured interest; isolated reform is absorbed; sincere moral appeal fails without changing anything.
- **Boundary:** the concept explains durability and entails **no remedy**; it states a default tendency, not an impossibility, which is why organisation rather than shared position is Ambedkar's route.

### H. The franchise, the body politic and the three-way separation

- **Enabled:** subordinated populations become constituencies; voice, bargaining, representation and policy attention become available.
- **Not dissolved:** candidate selection, patronage, symbolic mobilisation, social blocs, unequal social status, the marriage circle.
- **Why:** the franchise reaches the **citizen** at the ballot; endogamy, ritual rank and dependence reach the **household** continuously. An individualist premise does not by itself reach marriage and kinship.
- **Separation:** assertion (defensible) · supremacy (indefensible) · ordinary mobilisation (contains both; judge by **programme and effect**).
- **Closing move:** one person one vote and one vote one value against graded social worth is a contradiction a polity cannot indefinitely bear — a diagnosis of instability, not a prediction.
- **Conceded residue:** elite capture and permanent bloc politics are real risks inside assertion itself.

### I. The four dimensions and the two failure modes

| Dimension | Mechanism | Required response |
|---|---|---|
| Redistribution | unequal land, labour, education, networks, security | resources and institutional access |
| Recognition | stigma, purity/pollution, humiliation, graded worth | repudiation of inherited rank |
| Representation | others speak for the group; office access controlled elsewhere | autonomous voice and safeguards |
| Religion | hierarchy presented as sacred authority | freedom of conscience **plus** no civic disability |

**Mediating axes:** gender is **internal** to endogamy (caste and gender jointly structured, never arithmetically added); class **interacts** without exhausting (no reduction).

**Failure modes:** identity without redistribution leaves material power intact; redistribution without recognition preserves humiliation.

**Boundary:** the grid names distinctions and establishes **no priority** among them.

### J. Gandhi periodised, and the residue

| Phase | Content |
|---|---|
| Earlier | idealised hereditary duty-order distinguished from caste hierarchy; non-competitive, without superiority |
| Campaign | untouchability condemned; temple entry; sanitation work and common service; the term "Harijan," later rejected by many Dalit thinkers as paternalistic |
| Later | increasingly critical of birth-based barriers; increasingly supportive of inter-caste marriage |

⚠️ **Residue:** an idealised duty-order can allocate by birth **after** explicit ranking is denied — a genuine theoretical liability that chronology does not cancel.

⚠️ **Periodisation control:** a trajectory reconstructed across decades, not a claim that one date replaced every earlier commitment. **Term control:** "Harijan" is Gandhi's historical usage, contested then and since; never the narrator's neutral term.

### K. Gandhi's argument and method

**Five steps:** (1) all life participates in a moral-spiritual unity; (2) degradation violates non-violence and truth; (3) inherited exclusion corrupts **both** the excluded and the excluding conscience; (4) reform therefore requires repentance and changed conduct by the privileged; (5) legal prohibition is necessary and **cannot generate fellowship**.

**Strongest formulation:** untouchability is a sin against humanity and against religion itself, to be expiated by the practising community through penance, service and self-purification; no religious sanction can attach to the degradation of a human being.

**Six instruments:** change of heart and self-purification · truth-force (*satyāgraha*) · the constructive programme of education, sanitation, common service and village work · religious reinterpretation from within · non-violent persuasion rather than class war · trusteeship and economic reform to reduce dependence.

**Four presuppositions:** traditions contain resources for self-criticism · the privileged can be morally transformed · **means must embody the fellowship sought** · unity must not be purchased through coercive hatred.

**Achievement:** caste humiliation entered mass moral and political discourse; caste Hindu responsibility became central; reform was tied to everyday practice; political freedom was declared incomplete without internal moral reform.

**Diagnostic:** reaches the ritual moralisation of distance directly and material dependence partly; reaches the marriage circle only late; does **not** reach the sanction; does **not** address representation as an autonomous claim.

### L. Gandhi — four objections, four replies, four residues

| Objection | Reply | Residue |
|---|---|---|
| Paternalism: privileged reformers speak for and rename the oppressed | the moral responsibility of oppressors is indispensable and cannot be delegated | **stands unless** oppressed communities possess autonomous voice and leadership |
| Structural: heart-change leaves property, office and exclusion intact | the constructive programme and truth-force are social practices, not private sentiment | enforceable rights are still required; justice depending on benevolence is revocable |
| Duty-order: hereditary duty reproduces caste even without declared rank | the later trajectory weakens birth hierarchy and supports inter-caste marriage | the early defences remain a theoretical liability chronology cannot cancel |
| Religious reform: reinterpretation preserves the sanctifying authority | living ethical religion is distinguishable from unjust accretion | Ambedkar replies that the authority structure itself must be broken |

⚠️ **Differentiated verdict:** the replies succeed unequally — the "mere sentiment" charge fails, the duty-order reply partially succeeds, the paternalism reply establishes a real point without establishing sufficiency, and the structural objection survives essentially intact. **Insufficient rather than mistaken**, and insufficient precisely at rights and representation.

### M. Ambedkar — endogamy, 1916, and the gender bridge

**Four steps:** (1) a caste must preserve a closed marriage circle; (2) demographic imbalance threatens that closure; (3) practices regulating widows, widowers and marriage maintain it; (4) therefore caste reproduces through the control of sexuality and family, not occupation alone.

**Bridge:** caste continuity depends on regulating women's marriage and sexuality, so anti-caste and gender analysis are **jointly structured**; the full gender doctrine is routed to its own owner.

⚠️ **Limit:** the **origin** account is one hypothesis among several. Assert the **mechanism**, which survives every dispute about how the arrangement began.

**Use:** score every proposed remedy by asking whether it reaches the circle. Almost none does.

### N. Ambedkar — annihilation, 1936, and the five moves

**Five moves:** (1) caste is sustained by belief in the **sacred authority** of rules that rank and separate; (2) isolated reforms leave that normative foundation intact; (3) inter-dining alone does not reliably break the system; (4) **inter-caste marriage** attacks endogamy and creates social kinship; (5) annihilation requires rejection or radical reconstruction of scriptural authority, **social rights and material relations**.

**What annihilation is not:** removal of caste feeling by exhortation · abolition of cultural difference · a synonym for social harmony.

**Caste as anti-social and anti-national:** it narrows loyalty, blocks public spirit and makes collective action difficult; it creates **ranked separation**, not diversity. Cultural plurality allows interaction among **equal** groups. ⚠️ "Anti-national" names a disability in the conditions of common public life, never a fault of allegiance.

**Two-part protective test:** is the difference **ranked**? is association across it **free**? Plurality passes both; caste fails both.

⚠️ **Conceded limit:** state competence over religious belief is limited — which is why conversion and constitutional morality are inside the programme, not additions to it.

**2023 route:** social significance = the marriage circle, association and the valuation of persons; political significance = the sanction, representation and the conditions of democracy.

### O. Ambedkar — the triad, social democracy and constitutional morality

**Three dependencies:** liberty without equality permits domination · equality without liberty suppresses individuality · both without fraternity lack the social disposition to recognise others as equals.

**Fraternity:** the social basis of democracy — common associated life among social equals, supported by rights and institutions. The least attended and most necessary; cannot be legislated; no quota produces it.

**Political against social democracy:** periodic voting and formal citizenship, compatible with graded worth and therefore unstable · liberty, equality and fraternity as a way of life, democracy as a **mode of associated living**.

**Five moves from social power:** caste is enforced through social power, not merely private prejudice → victims need rights and remedies independent of dominant goodwill (**a withdrawable benefit is not a right**) → representation enables speaking for oneself → constitutional morality restrains inherited social authority, including a social majority → democracy must become associated living.

**Constitutional morality:** the form of administration can be perverted **without altering the text**, so a diffused public commitment must be cultivated; a safeguard is inert without the disposition that sustains it. ⚠️ Names a condition without specifying how it is produced.

### P. Beyond reservation, conversion, and the Marx contrast

❌ **The most damaging single error:** presenting Ambedkar as a theorist of reservation.

| Element | Why it exceeds a quota |
|---|---|
| Annihilation | reservation redistributes positions inside a hierarchy it does not dissolve |
| Endogamy | no quota touches the marriage circle |
| Constitutional morality | a safeguard is inert without the sustaining disposition |
| Social democracy | representation without it leaves the one-vote-one-value contradiction standing |
| Fraternity | cannot be legislated; no quota produces it |
| Educate, agitate, organise | reservation without organised capacity is a benefit **received**, not power **held** |
| Conversion (1956) | places the remedy outside the state altogether |
| State-directed economic power | political safeguards do not touch economic dependence |

**One-line thesis:** reservation addresses **access to positions**; annihilation addresses the **valuation of persons**, and only the second produces social democracy.

**Educate, agitate, organise:** critical self-respect · public contestation · autonomous collective power. **Not** self-help.

**Conversion, 1956:** ethical reconstruction around reason, compassion, equality and fraternity; both freedom of conscience and collective social critique; **not** a change of ritual label. ❌ Classical Buddhist doctrine belongs to another owner.

| Axis | Marxian | Ambedkarite |
|---|---|---|
| Hierarchy | ownership and class relation | caste status, endogamy, religion **and** class |
| Agency | the proletarian class | oppressed castes through rights and organisation |
| Method | revolutionary transformation | constitutional democracy plus social struggle |
| Ideal | classless society | social democracy of liberty, equality, fraternity |

⚠️ **Precisely:** economic power is accepted and the state must act on property; the **reduction of caste to class is denied**, because an economic revolution leaving the valuation of persons intact reproduces hierarchy in a new form.

**Three objections to Ambedkar:** legalism (reply: law changes power, protects action, enables movements; residue: implementation and evasion) · fragmentation (reply: unity built on the silence of the oppressed is domination; residue: elite capture, internal accountability) · religious hostility (reply: the target is authority insofar as it sanctifies graded inequality; residue: the doctrine/civic-disability line must be argued).

### Q. The debate, the 1932 dispute, affirmative action, the twenty traps and the optional residue

**The eight axes:** primary evil · hereditary duty-order · root mechanism · agent of reform · method · religion · democracy · main risk. Run both thinkers down each; never write two sequential biographies.

**Five convergences:** untouchability indefensible · equal worth and social fellowship · political freedom without social reform incomplete · institutions and conduct must both change · violence cannot found stable equality.

**Five irreducible disagreements:** is caste itself the disease? · can a hereditary duty-order be purified? · can reform rely on dominant-caste conscience? · are autonomous electoral safeguards required? · is exit from caste-sanctioning religion necessary?

⚠️ **Control:** convergence identifies common **ends** and does not equalise diagnostic adequacy or political power. A synthesis is defensible only when moral conversion **supports** autonomous rights and annihilation rather than postponing them.

⚠️ **Verdict:** Ambedkarite in structure and rights; Gandhian only where moral transformation serves — never substitutes for — annihilation.

**1932:** Communal Award (separate electoral arrangements) → Gandhi's opposition and fast → Poona Pact (reserved seats in joint electorates, a primary-election mechanism, an increased number of reserved seats). Gandhi feared permanent separation and fragmentation; Ambedkar feared representatives dependent on dominant-caste votes. **The issue is who may choose a subordinated group's representatives.** ⚠️ A compromise under morally and politically coercive circumstances — never a consensus. ❌ No figures or negotiating detail beyond this.

**Secular democracy:** Gandhi — equal regard for religions, non-violence and conscience → an **ethic of coexistence**. Ambedkar — constitutional morality, equal citizenship, freedom from religiously sanctioned hierarchy → an **institutional test**: no religious community may convert internal doctrine into **civic inferiority**.

**Village and modernity:** not tradition against modernity. Test both against **mobility, equal status, constitutional rights and exit from inherited occupation**. Centralised modern institutions can reproduce caste; local community can too. Neither scale is emancipatory without anti-caste power.

**Affirmative action — four grounds:** compensatory (terminates when transmitted disadvantage stops operating) · distributive/equal-opportunity (when disadvantage in access is removed) · representational (when institutions are genuinely representative) · anti-domination (when rank-based valuation ceases to organise social life). ⚠️ **The join:** compensatory in ground, distributive in mechanism, representational in institutional justification, anti-domination in aim.

**Reverse discrimination — five replies:** symmetry (the unreserved baseline is not neutral) · **merit as proxy** (merit is functional capacity; examination performance is a proxy whose reliability varies — contest the measure, not the value) · advantage, not guilt · group harm requires group remedy · **the conceded residue** — over-inclusion, and cost falling unevenly near the margin. **Stigma pair:** the stigma tracks the assumption that the unreserved baseline measured worth; representation is claimed as right and power, not concession.

**The classified Indian record:**

| Instrument | Type | What it is, exactly |
|---|---|---|
| Article 17 | constitutional rule | abolishes untouchability and forbids its practice; **not** a report that the practice ceased |
| SC/ST (Prevention of Atrocities) Act, 1989, in force 1990 | enacted statute | penal and protective |
| Second Backward Classes Commission, appointed 1979, reported 1980 | commission report | recommends; does not enact. Government action on OBC reservation in central government services followed in 1990 |
| *Indra Sawhney v. Union of India* (1992) | Supreme Court judgment | upheld OBC reservation under Article 16(4); directed creamy-layer exclusion; reservation ordinarily not to exceed 50 per cent save in extraordinary situations. **Nothing further** |
| Constitution (103rd Amendment) Act, 2019 | **enabling** amendment | inserted Articles 15(6) and 16(6); special provision including reservation up to ten per cent for economically weaker sections **other than** those already covered |
| *Janhit Abhiyan v. Union of India* (2022) | Supreme Court judgment | upheld the amendment's validity by a majority |

⚠️ **Creamy layer:** the internal filter answering over-inclusion; it concedes the group marker is a proxy. **Cost:** strengthens the distributive ground, weakens the purely representational one.

⚠️ **The 2019 philosophical point:** an economic criterion inside a framework built on social and educational backwardness reopens the foundational question — is the wrong **deprivation** or **ascriptive exclusion**? Make the argument without endorsing or condemning the amendment.

❌ **Four-stage caution:** enactment, notification, enforcement and social transformation are four different stages; none may be inferred from another, and none establishes that a philosophical justification is correct.

**The twenty traps, compressed:** 1 equating *varṇa*, *jāti*, caste and untouchability · 2 a timeless Gandhi · 3 inferring blanket anti-*varṇa* from anti-untouchability · 4 Ambedkar reduced to untouchability · 5 Ambedkar reduced to reservation · 6 caste reduced to class or occupation · 7 the Poona Pact narrated without autonomy or coercion · 8 "Harijan" treated as uncontested · 9 Article 17 or the 1989 Act read as proof caste ended · 10 Gandhi-as-conscience plus Ambedkar-as-law complementarity · 11 flattening the periodisation · 12 "Harijan" as the narrator's neutral term · 13 Ambedkar reduced to constitutional drafting · 14 educate–agitate–organise as self-help · 15 the Pact without the representation question · 16 romanticising village or central modernity · 17 collapsing caste into class or adding caste and gender arithmetically · 18 equating oppressed-caste assertion with hereditary supremacy · 19 treating redistribution, recognition and representation as substitutes · 20 letting convergence erase annihilation and autonomous rights.

**Optional-Advanced residue, recorded separately.** ⚠️ Four dossier debates remain enrichment only — moral reform against structural reconstruction; caste as status hierarchy against caste as labour order; constitutional morality against social fraternity; Ambedkar beyond Marx and Marx beyond Ambedkar — together with the legalism objection and its reply. ✅ No owned 2018–2025 part requires any of them, the dossier's promotion trigger has **not** fired against the held corpus, and the deployment rule is at most one item per 15- or 20-marker.

**Answer-length bands used throughout:** 10 marks about 150–220 words · 15 marks about 250–330 · 20 marks about 330–400.

THE THREE-STEP REPLY THAT ENDS THE DEFENCE
  1. EFFICIENCY CANNOT JUSTIFY ASCRIPTION. A division that ignores aptitude
     is not even efficient on its own terms.
  2. THE RANKING IS OF PERSONS. Caste subdivides the labouring population
     into hierarchically graded compartments, which no functional account of
     tasks requires or explains.
  3. THE STIGMA IS THE POINT. Where work is degrading BECAUSE OF WHO DOES IT,
     the hierarchy is doing the work, not the division.

        |
